# Claim 65 — Someone who couldn't afford health care under Barack Obama's Affordable Care Act gets penalized $10,000

### Did the Affordable Care Act, as signed into law by President Barack Obama, require most individuals to have qualifying health insurance coverage or pay a tax penalty?
Answer: Yes, the Affordable Care Act, as signed into law by President Barack Obama in 2010, included an 'individual mandate' requiring most Americans to obtain qualifying health insurance coverage or else face a tax penalty. The retrieved document adds that the penalty was capped at $695 per adult (or 2.5% of household income, whichever was greater) in its final years, and was reduced to $0 effective 2019 by the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017. The reasoner, working from memory alone, confirms the existence of the individual mandate and its tax-penalty requirement, which is a matter of broad public knowledge that it would be difficult to be wrong about.

Source URL: https://www.ftc.gov/about-ftc/history/presidential-visit/created

### What was the official name of the financial penalty imposed on individuals for not maintaining minimum essential coverage under the Affordable Care Act?
Answer: The official name of the financial penalty for not maintaining minimum essential coverage under the Affordable Care Act's individual mandate was the Individual Shared Responsibility Payment. The retrieved document confirms the penalty was called the 'Shared Responsibility Payment' (the commonly used shorter form), and the reasoner, whose reach is broad on this well-known policy, provides the full official name including 'Individual.'

Source URL: https://www.ftc.gov/about-ftc/history/presidential-visit/created

### For the 2014 tax year, what were the specific formulas used to calculate the penalty for not having health insurance under the Affordable Care Act?
Answer: For the 2014 tax year, the penalty for not having health insurance under the Affordable Care Act was calculated as the greater of a flat dollar amount or 1% of income above the tax filing threshold, subject to a cap based on the national average premium for a bronze-level plan. The retrieved document adds that the flat amount is per person with reduced rates for dependents under 18, and the family penalty is capped at 300% of the flat amount. The reasoner, recalling from broad internal knowledge, specifies the exact amounts: $95 per uninsured adult, $47.50 per uninsured child under 18, with a family maximum of $285, and income defined as modified adjusted gross income (MAGI).

Source URL: https://sgp.fas.org/crs/misc/R41331.pdf

### Was there a statutory maximum penalty amount for an individual under the Affordable Care Act's individual mandate, and if so, what was that maximum for a given tax year?
Answer: **CONFLICT.** The document store states that the statutory maximum penalty for an individual under the ACA's individual mandate was $10,000 per tax year, with tiered penalties based on income. The reasoner working from memory has no recollection of the exact statutory maximums but recalls that the penalty was capped at the national average premium for a bronze plan, which for many tax years was likely under $3,000, and thus infers it is not $10,000; this recollection reflects broad knowledge of the ACA's penalty structure that would be widely known.

Source URL: https://www.usac.org/lifeline/consumer-eligibility//created

### Did the Affordable Care Act provide an exemption from the tax penalty for individuals who could not afford health insurance based on a percentage of their household income?
Answer: Yes, the Affordable Care Act provided an exemption from the individual mandate tax penalty for individuals who could not afford health insurance based on a percentage of their household income. The retrieved document references 'the ACA's affordability exemption' and specifies that the threshold varied by year but was generally around 8% of income, applying to both employer-sponsored and marketplace coverage; the reasoner, working from memory, likewise reports that coverage costing more than 8% of household income triggered the exemption. The reasoner's answer aligns squarely with the documented record — this was a well-known provision of the ACA — establishing with substantial reach that those who genuinely could not afford coverage were specifically exempted from the penalty.

Answer: No Australian federal or state legislation was ever officially titled 'The Flora and Fauna Act'; the retrieved document confirms that the cited Victorian law is specifically the 'Flora and Fauna Guarantee Act 1988.' ### In what year did Australian citizens vote in a national referendum that pertained to the rights or classification of Aboriginal Australians?
Answer: Australian citizens voted in a national referendum on 27 May 1967 concerning the rights and constitutional status of Aboriginal Australians. The retrieved document adds that it was the most-decisive referendum victory aimed at removing discriminatory references from the Constitution, and the reasoner from memory reports that it successfully amended the Constitution to allow the Commonwealth to legislate for Aboriginal people and to count them in the national census.

Source URL: https://theconversation.com/right-wrongs-write-yes-what-was-the-1967-referendum-all-about-76512

### What was the primary question or constitutional change put to voters in the 1967 Australian referendum?
Answer: The primary constitutional change at the 1967 Australian referendum was to amend the Constitution so that the Commonwealth Parliament could make laws for Aboriginal people and ensure they were counted in national population statistics. The retrieved document specifies this was done by deleting "...other than the aboriginal people in any State..." from section 51(xxvi) and removing the entirety of section 127, which had excluded Aboriginal people from population counts. The reasoner, working from memory alone, recalls the same substantive change with high reach, as this is a broadly known landmark in Australian constitutional history.

Answer: **CONFLICT.** The 1967 Australian referendum did not directly repeal or modify any act named 'The Flora and Fauna Act.' The reasoner working from memory alone reports having no recollection of any act named 'The Flora and Fauna Act' in Australian history and states this is widely considered a myth; given that a law of this nature and significance would be broadly known, this denial carries substantial weight. The two accounts thus offer conflicting assessments of whether the act existed at all, while agreeing the 1967 referendum did not directly repeal or modify it.

### What specific legal changes regarding Aboriginal Australians were achieved through the 1967 referendum or other legislative acts in the 1960s?
Answer: The 1967 referendum achieved two specific constitutional changes: amending Section 51(xxvi) to grant the federal Parliament power to make laws with respect to Aboriginal people (by removing the exclusionary phrase '…other than the aboriginal people in any State…'), and repealing Section 127, which had stated that 'aboriginal natives should not be counted,' thereby allowing Aboriginal people to be included in population statistics. The retrieved document specifies that the legislation was the Constitution Alteration (Aboriginals) 1967.
